Were I rich, I would spend all my money to the last farthing pour la propagande de cette divine vérité. But my means are very poor, and I am obliged to live by my work, by translating and writing in the papers.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-077" href="#sec-07-fn-077" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-077" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In later letters she wrote :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>I was in deepest darkness, but I have seen the light, and to this light I have given myself up entirely.  Spiritism is a great truth, and I will serve it to the grave. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>For spiritism I am ready to work night and day, so long as I have a morsel of bread, and that only because it is hard to work when one is hungry. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>I have already sacrificed myself for spiritualism, and in defence of my faith and the truth I am ready at any moment to lay my head on the block. . . .<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-078" href="#sec-07-fn-078" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-078" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>If you hear that the sinful Blavatsky has perished, not in the bloom of years and beauty, by some surprising death, and that she has dematerialised ‘for ever,’ then you will know that it is for spiritualism.  In thee, Lord, do we put our trust, and we shall not be confounded for ever. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>I have quite ceased to get any letters from my aunts and sisters ; they have evidently all forgotten me, and so much the better for them.  I am no credit to them, to tell the truth. I shall now never go back again to Russia.  My father is dead, nobody wants me, and I am altogether superfluous in the world. Here I am at least a human being ; there, I am — Blavatsky. I know that everybody respects me here, and I am needed for spiritualism.  Now the spirits are my brothers and sisters, my father and mother.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-079" href="#sec-07-fn-079" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-079" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">From her letters it is plain that Olcott used every possible means to bring her into notoriety and popularity, raising her to the rank of Countess, mixing her up with “princes, boyards and imaginary governors-general,”<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-080" href="#sec-07-fn-080" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-080" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> and making her out a second Livingstone in her travels in Africa and the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-257" data-page="257" data-folio="217" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n256_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 217">[p. 217]</button> Soudan;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-081" href="#sec-07-fn-081" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-081" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and she did him a like service. While the vogue of spiritualism lasted, things went well. Everything that they wrote was widely read, and they rose steadily in public estimation. There was a spirit who was peculiarly friendly with her. Here is what she says about him :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>My John King alone is a sufficient recompense for all ; he is a host in himself to me. And yet they call him the double of the medium, him and Crookes’s Katie King. What sort of double can he be when the medium Williams is not here at all, but John King in his own person, with his own black beard and his white Chinese saucer-upside-down cap, going about here in America from one medium to another, and doing me the honour of visiting me incessantly, though he has not the least resemblance to me ? No, John King is a personality, a definite, living, spiritual personality. Whether devil or good spirit, he is at all events a spirit, and not the medium’s prototype.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-082" href="#sec-07-fn-082" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-082" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Olcott tells us that she had known John King since 1860, and had seen him and talked with him in different countries.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-083" href="#sec-07-fn-083" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-083" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But a peculiarly odious piece of fraudulent spiritism was exposed early in 1875, and public interest in the subject began to die down. The comrades tried various plans to keep their hold on the people, but it was useless. On May 24th, Madame Blavatsky writes :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Disaster has come upon us. Dr. Child has appeared in the character of the spiritist Antichrist, and, as the Judas of the seven councils, has destroyed spiritualism. Even the most advanced spiritualists begin to be afraid of public opinion, and their ‘high respectability’ induces many to continue to believe in spirits in secret only, and privately. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>I am ready to give my life for the spread of the sacred truth. Olcott is helping me as much as he can, both with his pen and with pecuniary sacrifices for the cause. He is as passionately <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-258" data-page="258" data-folio="218" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n257_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 218">[p. 218]</button> devoted to spiritism as I am. But he is far from rich and has nothing to live on but his literary labours, and he has to keep a wife and a whole lot of children. Olcott is sitting on heaps of his <em>People from the Other World</em>, like Marius on the ruins of Carthage, and thinking bitter things. Not a thousand copies of his book have been sold in five months.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-084" href="#sec-07-fn-084" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-084" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the 18th of July she writes again:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Here, you see, is my trouble, to-morrow there will be nothing to eat. Something quite out of the way must be invented. It is doubtful if Olcott’s ‘Miracle Club’ will help; I will fight to the last.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-085" href="#sec-07-fn-085" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-085" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Things were in a very bad way. Spiritualism was worked out, and the partners were threatened with want. Some new source of income had to be found. The Miracle Club was clearly meant to be something new and startling to catch public attention. But it did not succeed. Her letter of the 10th September is still very despondent.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">4. Such were the circumstances in which the Theosophical Society was founded. Colonel Olcott gives us the dates and the steps in the following passage :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>The formation of such a society was suggested by myself on the evening of September 7th, 1875, in the rooms of Madame Blavatsky, at 46 Irving Place, New York City, where a small gathering of her friends had assembled to listen to a discourse by a Mr. G. H. Felt on the lost canon of proportion of the Ancient Egyptians. My views as to the necessity of such a society were embodied in a short impromptu address and, receiving general assent, a motion was made by Mr. W. Q. Judge and adopted, <em>nem. con.</em>, that I be elected chairman of the meeting, and on my motion Mr. Judge was elected secretary. A committee to frame Bye-laws was chosen. A report of the proceedings including a digest of my little speech, was published in a local daily paper, copied into the <em>Spiritual Scientist</em>, of Boston, and thence transferred by Mrs. E. H. Britten into her large <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-259" data-page="259" data-folio="219" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n258_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 219">[p. 219]</button> work, “Nineteenth Century Miracles” (p. 296), where the curious reader may find it in detail. No previous consultation had been held about the matter between Madame Blavatsky and myself or any body else; the suggestion was entirely unpremeditated and grew out of the discussion provoked by Mr. Felt’s lecture. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the 17th November, the Society was launched as a perfected organization.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-086" href="#sec-07-fn-086" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-086" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Olcott became President, Judge Vice-president, and Madame Blavatsky Corresponding Secretary. To her friend in Russia Madame Blavatsky wrote on the 20th of September:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Olcott is now organising the Theosophical Society in New York. It will be composed of learned occultists and cabbalists, of philosophes Hermétiques of the nineteenth century, and of passionate antiquaries and Egyptologists generally. We want to make an experimental comparison between spiritualism and the magic of the ancients by following literally the instructions of the old Cabbalas, both Jewish and Egyptian. I have for many years been studying la philosophie Hermétique in theory and practice, and am every day coming to the conclusion that spiritualism in its physical manifestations is nothing but the Python of Paracelsus, <em>i.e.</em>, the intangible ether which Reichenbach calls Od. The Pythonesses of the ancients used to magnetise themselves—read Plutarch and his account of the oracular currents, read Cornelius Agrippa, Paracelsus, the Magia Adamica of Eugenius Philalethes, and others. You will always see better, and can communicate with the spirits by this means<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-086-2" href="#sec-07-fn-086" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-086" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>— self-magnetisation.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-087" href="#sec-07-fn-087" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-087" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On December 6th she wrote:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>It is the same spiritualism, but under another name. Now you will see if we shall not start the most learned investigations. Our vice-treasurer, Newton, is a millionare, and president of the New York spiritualists.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-088" href="#sec-07-fn-088" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-088" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-260"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="260" data-folio="220" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n259_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 220">[p. 220]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">These are most instructive paragraphs. It is, above all, to be noted that the purpose of the Theosophical Society is “to make an experimental comparison between spiritualism and the magic of the ancients.” There is as yet no mention of Buddhism or Hinduism. There is no suggestion that the foundress receives her wisdom in ample measure, without trouble, through “Masters” from the ancient sources. She still struggles forward by experimental comparison; and her occult communications are not with living Masters in Tibet, but with the spirits of the dead. “Mahātma Morya” has not yet appeared above the horizon. “John King” is still “the Master of her dreams.”<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-089" href="#sec-07-fn-089" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-089" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The facts are simple and natural. Madame Blavatsky had been a medium from childhood, and had practised spiritualism since 1858, if not from an earlier date, though it does not appear that she ever worked as a hired medium. She started a spiritualist show in Cairo in 1872. She lived by spiritualist writing, and made the most serious protestations of belief in spiritualism from 1873 to September, 1875. The Miracle Club and the Theosophic Society were successive attempts to start something new and successful, when public interest in spiritualism declined. Theosophical doctrine at a later date became a blend of Buddhism, Hinduism and various forms of occultism; but, when first launched, it was merely an addition of the magic and mysticism of Egypt and of mediæval Judaism to spiritualism, with a view to stimulating the jaded appetite of the people of New York.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is clear that she had been interested to some extent in all these mysterious things for years. She was a woman of most unusual temperament, possessing the powers of the medium, the clairvoyant, the clairaudient and probably also of the automatic writer. She had met “Eliphaz Lévy” in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-261" data-page="261" data-folio="221" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n260_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 221">[p. 221]</button> Paris; and she had probably given some attention to juggling, devil-dancing and such like in Egypt and the East. The following sentences are probably quite reliable. We should not have had this curious passage in her letters at all, had it not been that her correspondent took in the American papers, and she felt she must apologize once more for Colonel Olcott’s outrageous exaggerations :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>In a detailed account of the story of Katie King Olcott makes out of me something mysteriously terrible, and almost leads the public to suspect that I have either sold my soul to the devil or am the direct heiress of Count Germain and Cagliostro. Do not believe it; I have merely learnt in Egypt and Africa, in India and in the East generally, a great deal of what other people do not know. I have made friends with dervishes, and I do indeed belong to one mystic society, but it does not follow that I have become an Apollonius of Tyana in petticoats.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-090" href="#sec-07-fn-090" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-090" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">She now began to study modern works on occultism seriously. About the same time she began to draw away from her old full belief in spiritualism and to hint that it was not spirits, but merely “ shells ” that caused the marvels. This theory comes from “ Eliphaz Lévy.” He taught that when a man dies, the spirit departs completely, leaving behind in this world only an empty “ shell,” which, however, has the power of producing phenomena.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Five months before the foundation of the Theosophical Society, on the third of April, 1875, Madame Blavatsky married in Philadelphia an Armenian, a Russian subject, named Michael Bettalay.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-091" href="#sec-07-fn-091" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-091" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Yet N. V. Blavatsky was still <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-262" data-page="262" data-folio="222" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n261_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 222">[p. 222]</button> alive; and there had been no divorce. It was a case of bigamy pure and simple. Doubtless she said she was a widow; for she practised that piece of deceit for many years. She put down her age in the marriage-register as thirty-six, while she was actually forty-three.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-092" href="#sec-07-fn-092" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-092" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The new society was scarcely started when serious trouble arose from her old spiritualist allies; for they felt that she was faithless to them. She had publicly declared that the spirits had brought her a medal and clasp from her father’s grave, and Olcott had published in his <em>People from the Other World</em> a drawing of the medal and clasp. This enabled the medium Home to trace her antecedents and to obtain information about her private life. He had also got to the bottom of some of her fraudulent spiritualistic phenomena. He then attacked her publicly on both counts.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-093" href="#sec-07-fn-093" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-093" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The new society went fairly well for a time, and then interest steadily waned. Yet the comrades held on, never allowing the organization to fall to pieces.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-094" href="#sec-07-fn-094" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-094" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">5. For two years Madame Blavatsky toiled at her new studies, and on the 2nd of October, 1877, her <em>Isis Unveiled</em> was published. It is a really noteworthy book, and that for two reasons. First, it was the earliest vigorous attempt made to defend the ancient religions against the harsh judgments still only too common at the time. Secondly, it took up a striking attitude to that great shady border-land which lies between jugglery and religion. Everything mysterious, weird, occult or magical, the unexplored powers of the human mind, and all suggestive, or symbolic words, acts or things, had an overpowering fascination for her. It <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-263" data-page="263" data-folio="223" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n262_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 223">[p. 223]</button> is also clear that at a fairly early date she began to realize, in a more or less hazy way, certain facts which science has only recently perceived and acknowledged. The most important of these are (<em>a</em>) that spiritualism, clairvoyance, hypnotic trances, faith-healing and many of the phenomena of dreams and apparitions are, in essentials, identical with practices and occurrences which are vouched for in the literature of Classical and early Christian times, and with much which happens among modern savages; (<em>b</em>) that a considerable proportion of the marvels are genuine, whatever the ultimate explanation of their reality may be; and (<em>c</em>) that those who make such practices their profession sooner or later have recourse to fraud.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-095" href="#sec-07-fn-095" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-095" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> In the <em>Isis</em> these questions are not raised or treated in any scholarly fashion; and the evidence, good, bad and indifferent, is simply thrown down in indiscriminate heaps; so that the book as it stands is practically of no scientific value; yet the personal knowledge the authoress had of many of the practices dealt with, and her perception that there was something genuine in them, gave the book a certain value, and made it very attractive to many people.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">One of the most notable characteristics of the book is its violent polemic against modern science and Christianity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The authoress so wrote as to lead her readers to understand that she was a woman of vast learning, and that she had mastered all the great works on occultism in existence; while the truth is that all the learning it contains is borrowed, or rather stolen, from modern books; for in most cases there is no acknowledgment. Mr. Wm. Emmette Coleman of San Francisco spent three years in making an exhaustive analysis of the contents of Madame Blavatsky's writings. The following is his statement with regard to the <em>Isis</em> : <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-096" href="#sec-07-fn-096" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-096" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-264"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="264" data-folio="224" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n263_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 224">[p. 224]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By careful analysis I found that in compiling <em>Isis</em> about 100 books were used. About 1,400 books are quoted from and referred to in this work; but, from the 100 books which its author possessed, she copied everything in <em>Isis</em> taken from and relating to the other 1,300. There are in <em>Isis</em> about 2,100 quotations from and references to books that were copied, at second-hand, from books other than the original, and of this number only about 140 are credited to the books from which Madame Blavatsky copied them at second-hand, The others are quoted in such a manner as to lead the reader to think that Madame Blavatsky had read and utilized the original works, and had quoted from them at first-hand, — the truth being that these originals had evidently never been read by Madame Blavatsky.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Col. Olcott stated in the <em>Theosophist</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-097" href="#sec-07-fn-097" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-097" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> that Madame Blavatsky’s library contained about 100 books when she wrote the <em>Isis</em>; so that Mr. Coleman’s critical judgment is confirmed. The following is a list of the books from which the largest numbers of quotations were taken :<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-098" href="#sec-07-fn-098" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-098" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-004"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-004 · FOLIO 264</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/modern-religious-movements-india/datasets/tables/tbl-004_p0264.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/modern-religious-movements-india/datasets/tidy/tbl-004_p0264.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"><span class="sc">Passages</span></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Dunlap’s <em>Sod: the Son of the Man</em></td><td class="num">134</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ennemoser’s <em>History of Magic</em>, English Trans.</td><td class="num">107</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Demonologia</em></td><td class="num">85</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Dunlap’s <em>Spirit History of Man</em></td><td class="num">77</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Salverte’s <em>Philosophy of Magic</em>, English Trans.</td><td class="num">68</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Dunlap’s <em>Sod: the Mysteries of Adoni</em></td><td class="num">65</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Des Mousseaux’s <em>Magie au Dix-neuvième Siècle</em></td><td class="num">63</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Des Mousseaux’s <em>Hauts Phénomènes de la Magie</em></td><td class="num">45</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>King’s <em>Gnostics</em>, 1st edition</td><td class="num">42</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Supernatural religion</em></td><td class="num">40</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Mackenzie’s <em>Masonic Cyclopaedia</em></td><td class="num">36</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Zeller’s <em>Plato and the Old Academy</em></td><td class="num">35</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There are some students who, while recognizing frankly that the bulk of the <em>Isis</em> is built out of materials from modern works, are yet inclined to think that it may be true, as was stated by Madame Blavatsky and Col. Olcott, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-265" data-page="265" data-folio="225" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n264_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 225">[p. 225]</button> that large sections of the book were written automatically. If this be true, then the explanation must be that her sub-conscious mind had retained all that she had read on these subjects, and gave out the materials when each fit of automatic writing came on.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The book contains innumerable errors, many of them of the most rudimentary type. The commonest Sanskrit words are misspelt; the Buddhist doctrine of transmigration is grossly misrepresented; and the <em>Bhagavadgītā</em> is confused with the <em>Bhāgavata Purāṇa</em>. The following sentences give a sample of the scholarship of the book :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Apart from the now-discovered fact that the whole story of such a massacre of the Innocents is bodily taken from the Hindu <em>Bagavedgitta</em>, and Brahmanical traditions, the legend refers, moreover, allegorically to an historical fact. King Herod is the type of Kansa, the tyrant of Madura.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-099" href="#sec-07-fn-099" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-099" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yet, to-day, we are asked to believe that all this is the wisdom of the Mahātmas. When Madame Blavatsky went to India, an elaborate myth was created, to the effect that for many years she had been receiving her wisdom from these Masters in Tibet. Thus all who accept this myth are compelled to explain the <em>Isis</em> as an early exposition of orthodox Theosophy. As a matter of fact, it represents the state of the writer's mind in 1877 : it does not teach the doctrine of reincarnation;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-100" href="#sec-07-fn-100" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-100" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> it teaches that man is a being of a threefold nature, while the orthodox doctrine makes him sevenfold; there is no mention of the great doctrine of brotherhood; and a great deal of the furious attack on Christianity is contrary to the professed standards of to-day.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">6. About the time when the <em>Isis</em> was published, Home's <em>Lights and Shadows of Spiritualism</em> also appeared, and its exposures of her frauds agitated her so much, and influenced public opinion so seriously, that she decided to leave America <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-266" data-page="266" data-folio="226" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n265_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 226">[p. 226]</button> for ever and go to India. Here is how she wrote in December, 1877, two months after the publication of the <em>Isis</em>:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>It is for this that I am going for ever to India, and for very shame and vexation I want to go where no one will know my name. Home’s malignity has ruined me for ever in Europe.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-101" href="#sec-07-fn-101" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-101" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Home’s evidence must have been irrefragable; for Olcott did not attempt to meet it, though asked to do so.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-102" href="#sec-07-fn-102" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-102" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In anticipation of their voyage to India, Olcott wrote to a Hindu friend, whom he had met some time before on a voyage across the Atlantic, and through him got into correspondence with Svāmī Dayānanda Sarasvatī, the founder of the Ārya Samāj. As a result of an interchange of letters, the two societies were connected the one with the other. This continued after the Theosophists reached India; but finally they separated in anger.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-103" href="#sec-07-fn-103" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-103" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the 25th of May, 1878, Madame Blavatsky was divorced from her Armenian husband.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-104" href="#sec-07-fn-104" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-104" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> Olcott says that the husband obtained the divorce on the ground of desertion.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-105" href="#sec-07-fn-105" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-105" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">7. In December, 1878, “the Theosophical Twins,” as Madame Blavatsky had named herself and Olcott, sailed from New York. They arrived in Bombay in January; and that city, for almost three years, was the headquarters of the society. Madame Coulomb and her husband, who had meantime lost all their money, reached Bombay late in the spring of 1880, and were established at headquarters as friends and assistants of Madame Blavatsky.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The opinions and the teaching of the Twins now became much more distinctively Indian than they had been in America. They declared themselves Buddhists, and entered into close relations with Buddhism in Ceylon.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Theosophic Myth also began to take definite shape.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-267"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="267" data-folio="227" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n266_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 227">[p. 227]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They diligently taught the existence of the Great White Brotherhood and their Lodge in Tibet. The theory took shape gradually, and some of the more showy parts have been added only recently. The completed myth is as follows: A large number of men have reached the stage of Adepts in the Wisdom; and many have become members of the Hierarchy which governs this world.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-106" href="#sec-07-fn-106" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-106" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> These beings are far beyond death and transmigration; yet they live upon earth, mostly in Tibet; and a few of them are willing to take as apprentices those who have resolved to devote themselves to humanity. Since they take pupils, they are known as Masters. On account of their greatness they are called Mahātmas, great souls. Madame Blavatsky, we are told, was selected from the whole human race in our days to receive the ancient wisdom from these Masters. Her own particular master was Mahātma Morya; but Koot Hoomi and others were also ready to help. From them she received Theosophy: it was in no sense her own creation. As far back as 1851 she had met Mahātma Morya, “the Master of her dreams”;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-107" href="#sec-07-fn-107" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-107" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> she had spent seven years in unremitting study in Tibet; and in the intervening years the wisdom had been poured into her mind in amplest measure.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Our narrative has provided sufficient disproof of the myth. As late as 1874 she was neither Buddhist nor Theosophist, but a Spiritualist, and was ready at any moment to lay her head on the block in defence of her faith.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-108" href="#sec-07-fn-108" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-108" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> Instead of learning from a living Master, she was the confidant of a disembodied spirit, John King.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-109" href="#sec-07-fn-109" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-109" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> Even when the Theosophical Society was founded, there was no mention of India but only of the Kabbala and the Hermetic system.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-110" href="#sec-07-fn-110" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-110" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-268"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="268" data-folio="228" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n267_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 228">[p. 228]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The two travelled a great deal in various parts of India, and were usually received by the Hindu community with acclamation. The society steadily grew in numbers and popularity, largely as a result of the new theory of the Masters. For, wherever they went, miraculous events, which they called “ phenomena,” appeared; and Madame Blavatsky attributed all to her Masters, or to the occult knowledge she had derived from them. If some prominent European were inquiring about Theosophy, a letter from Koot Hoomi would be sure to fall on his head. Telegrams from the Masters would come tumbling through the air — “precipitated” in Theosophic phrase — but, strangely enough, bearing the stamp of the British Telegraph office. The Masters shewed themselves now and then in one of their bodies to selected people. Lost articles were found, and new things arrived in unheard-of ways. Half a cigarette, or a lock of Madame Blavatsky’s hair, would be transported from one place to another by “ occult ” means. Probably a percentage of the phenomena were genuine, as we should expect in the case of a woman of Madame Blavatsky’s powers; but no carefully sifted evidence has ever been given for any of them; while evidence exists which proves clearly that many of them were fraudulent; and, as to the Masters, nothing worth the name of evidence has ever been produced for their existence.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-111" href="#sec-07-fn-111" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-111" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">8. One of the most famous occurrences took place at Simla. There was a dinner-party there one evening, in the house of Mr. A. O. Hume, a distinguished Indian Civilian, holding very high office under Government. After dinner it was proposed that Madame Blavatsky should give an example of her powers. After some talk she asked Mrs. Hume whether she had lost anything she would like very much to recover. In reply she described <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-269" data-page="269" data-folio="229" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n268_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 229">[p. 229]</button> a brooch, which some little time before had passed out of the family. Madame Blavatsky indicated a spot in the garden where they might look for it. They looked, and the brooch was found.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. and Mrs. Hume accepted the occurrence as a genuine occult phenomenon. It was described in glowing terms in the papers; and it has been continuously used by Theosophists ever since as evidence of the truth of their system. Yet the explanation is simple and undeniable. The truth came out in the following way:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>The publication of the incident in the <em>Pioneer</em> gave rise to a good deal of discussion in the daily papers of the period. The <em>Englishman</em> pointed out a number of awkward lacunæ in the account given, and was especially anxious to know something of the “person” who had allowed the brooch “to pass out of their possession.” It remarked —</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“There is nothing to show to whom Mrs. Hume’s friend, to whom she had given the brooch, parted with it. It might have been to some one who had communicated the fact and given the brooch to Madame. A very slight hint in the conversation might have turned Mrs. Hume’s thoughts, almost unconsciously, towards her lost brooch,” etc.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>The <em>Bombay Gazette</em>, of October 13th, 1880, after noticing this article, went on —</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“We can furnish the <em>Englishman</em> with a small item of intelligence. At the end of last and the commencement of this year, a young gentleman who had resided at Simla previously, and was, we believe, well known to the Hume family, sojourned for some months in Bombay, and was part of the time a guest of Madame Blavatsky at Girgaum. The latter lady’s connection with this gentleman may or may not have had anything to do with the affair of the brooch, though to our mind it is as probable as that the presence of the brooch in the flower bed was due to ‘occult’ phenomena.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Three days later a correspondent of <em>The Times of India</em> wrote —</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“It may interest some of your readers on the other side of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-270" data-page="270" data-folio="230" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n269_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 230">[p. 230]</button> India to learn that some months ago an individual who had been immediately connected with some of the members of Mr. Hume’s family at Simla arrived in Bombay. He was, I believe, hospitably received by Madame Blavatsky, if, indeed, he did not spend some weeks at her house in Girgaum, and when he left for England eventually, the arrangements for his passage were made through the agency of Colonel Olcott.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">All this is very suggestive; but still more so is a pretty idyll narrated by the <em>Civil and Military Gazette</em> a month or two later :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Once upon a time a certain Daphnis had received as a gage d’amour from his Chloe, a brooch, an ancestral gem, formerly the property of Chloe’s Mamma, which probably poor Chloe considered would in the course of happy time revert to her possession, when Daphnis and all that was his should be her own. But the course of true love never did run smooth, and the unhappy Daphnis, separated from Chloe, and driven by impecuniosity, deposited his pretty gift with an accommodating pawn-broker — for a consideration — meaning doubtless in future time to redeem the precious pledge. The trinket chanced, however, to attract the notice of a very famous spiritualist and medium, a lady who dealt in mysteries of psychic force and powers of disintegration and reintegration of matter. There is nothing to prevent a spiritualist, however magically endowed, from dealing also in mundane affairs after the usual humdrum and worldly fashion, and in this instance the famous lady chose to achieve the possession of the object of her fancy by the ordinary method of paying for it. Time rolled on, and it happened in the fulness thereof that the celebrated medium and Chloe’s Mamma became acquainted, and under some circumstances, which attained perhaps an undeserved notoriety, the brooch became again the property of its original possessor.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-112" href="#sec-07-fn-112" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-112" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> ”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Two further points came out after this account was printed. Mr. Hormusji Seervai, a Bombay jeweller, saw an account of the miracle in the papers, and realized from the description of the brooch that he had repaired it for Madame <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-271" data-page="271" data-folio="231" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n270_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 231">[p. 231]</button> Blavatsky.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-113" href="#sec-07-fn-113" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-113" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Finally, the Rev. George Patterson, when on a visit to Bombay at the end of 1884, learned that Madame Blavatsky bought the pawn-ticket from the young man and redeemed the trinket.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There cannot be the slightest question as to the truth of the explanation ; for not one of the facts has ever been so much as questioned. Mr. Hume himself publicly acknowledged that the famous phenomenon was a piece of well-planned fraud. Yet the Theosophical Society still uses this fraud, indefensible and undefended, as an example of occult agency.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-114" href="#sec-07-fn-114" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-114" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">9. The Theosophic conception of the world, man and religion, which is nowhere given in the <em>Isis</em>, now gradually took shape. A brief analysis of the system is given below.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-115" href="#sec-07-fn-115" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-115" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> The main channel through which the fresh teaching found its way to Theosophists and the public was a series of long letters, which Madame Blavatsky averred were written and sent by the Master known as Koot Hoomi. Parts of these letters were published by Mr. Sinnett, an Englishman who was editor of the <em>Pioneer</em> and had become a Theosophist, in his books, <em>Esoteric Buddhism</em> and <em>The Occult World</em> ; but much of the material was so poor that it had to be eliminated as rubbish.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-116" href="#sec-07-fn-116" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-116" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>The Occult World</em> was published in June, 1881. Mr. H. Kiddle of New York read the volume, and discovered in one of the letters a long passage copied almost verbatim from an address delivered by him at Lake Pleasant, August 15, 1880, and reported the same month in <em>The Banner of Light</em>. The date of the letter was two months later. When this was made public, a ridiculous reply, purporting to come <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-272" data-page="272" data-folio="232" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n271_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 232">[p. 232]</button> from the Mahātma, was published, but no one was deceived. It was a case of deliberate plagiarism; and the final proof that it was so is found in the fact that in the more recent editions of <em>The Occult World</em> the passage is omitted.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-117" href="#sec-07-fn-117" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-117" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">10. In December, 1882, the headquarters of the society were moved to Adyar, Madras. The Coulombs went along with the rest of the staff. M. Coulomb was Librarian, while his wife was Assistant Corresponding Secretary of the society. Besides that, Madame Coulomb acted as housekeeper, while her husband took charge of all repairs or additions to the buildings.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Madame Blavatsky occupied a large upper room in the main bungalow. See plan <em>B</em> on page 235. Early in 1883 a new room for occult purposes was built against the west wall of her room. There were two windows in the west wall. The south window, transformed into a door, became the ordinary entrance into the new room, which was called the Occult Room. The north window was removed, and a single layer of bricks filled up the aperture on the Occult Room side, leaving a recess about 15 in. deep on the other side, in Madame Blavatsky’s bedroom. Part of the Occult Room was screened off by means of a curtain to form a small room for the Shrine. This was a wooden cupboard which, by means of two stout wires, was hung on the wall over the thin brick partition where the north window had been. In the Shrine was placed a portrait of the Master, Koot Hoomi. The doors of the Shrine were occasionally thrown open to Theosophists, that they might see the master’s portrait. Hindus bowed reverently <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-273" data-page="273" data-folio="233" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n272_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 233">[p. 233]</button> before him and burned incense to him.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-118" href="#sec-07-fn-118" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-118" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Both Indians and Europeans were accustomed to present their requests in the form of letters. The door would then be shut; and, when it was re-opened, a reply from the Master would be found within it. On one occasion a broken saucer was put in beside him. When the Shrine was re-opened, it was found intact. From this time onward many of the most striking phenomena were connected with the Shrine.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By the year 1884 the Theosophical Society had attained great proportions. There were over a hundred branches in India, and Hindus everywhere rejoiced in its work. Nor is their enthusiasm hard to understand. Theosophy provided a new defence of Hinduism for the thousands of educated men whose Western education had filled them with shivering doubts about their religion. It condemned Christian missionaries as impudent and ignorant intruders, who dared to criticize Hinduism and Buddhism, the two faiths which alone among all the religions of the world still taught clearly the truths of the Ancient Wisdom. All the great and good of every age had known and taught this wisdom; but, while it had been lost or beclouded elsewhere, Hinduism and Buddhism still retained its priceless principles; and in Tibet lived immortal teachers who were now, through Madame Blavatsky, revealing the Wisdom in all its glory to the whole human race. Yet even this most flattering proclamation would not have won its way as it did apart from the phenomena. There can be no question that it was these marvels that trumpeted the cause throughout India, and convinced the Hindu of the truth of the new propaganda.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">11. In 1884 a great crisis in the history of Theosophy occurred. As Theosophists still assert that the whole was a missionary plot, and that Madame Blavatsky came out of it triumphant, we cannot dismiss it in a paragraph. In order <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-276" data-page="276" data-folio="236" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n275_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 236">[p. 236]</button> to place our readers in a position to judge for themselves, we shall give, in as brief a form as possible, an orderly outline of the significant events of the crisis and shall also indicate where the detailed evidence produced on both sides may be seen and examined.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-274"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="274" data-folio="234" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n273_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 234">[p. 234]</button></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-022"><img src="/figures/modern-religious-movements-india/fig-022_p0274.webp" alt="A. JUDGE’S PLAN, ORIGINAL SIZE" loading="lazy"><figcaption><em>A.</em> JUDGE’S PLAN, ORIGINAL SIZE</figcaption></figure><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-275"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="275" data-folio="235" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n274_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 235">[p. 235]</button></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-023"><img src="/figures/modern-religious-movements-india/fig-023_p0275.webp" alt="B. HODGSON’S PLAN, ⅖ OF ORIGINAL SIZE — PLAN OF OCCULT ROOM WITH SHRINE AND SURROUNDINGS (From measurements taken by R. Hodgson, assisted by statements of Theosophic witnesses)" loading="lazy"><figcaption><em>B.</em> HODGSON’S PLAN, ⅖ OF ORIGINAL SIZE — PLAN OF OCCULT ROOM WITH SHRINE AND SURROUNDINGS (From measurements taken by R. Hodgson, assisted by statements of Theosophic witnesses)</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>a</em>. On the 21st of February, 1884, Madame Blavatsky, Colonel Olcott and a young Calcutta Brāhman, Mohini Mohan Chatterji, sailed from Bombay for Europe. By Madame Blavatsky’s explicit instructions, the Coulombs were left in charge of her rooms at the headquarters, Madras. They were to reside in them, and to look after her furniture and dogs. No one was to disturb them. There is the best evidence possible for these statements. The written instructions have been published;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-119" href="#sec-07-fn-119" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-119" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and the following is a letter written by Madame Blavatsky, and printed in Dr. Hartmann’s pamphlet published in September:<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-120" href="#sec-07-fn-120" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-120" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p><span class="sc">46 Rue Notre Dame des Champs, Paris,</span> April, 2–84. She swore to me that she would take care of my rooms, only asking me to let it be known that she alone had the right over all, and would have and keep the key. Having told Dr. Hartmann that he was welcome to my books and my desk in my absence, she made a vow when alone with me, and declared that if I allowed one single person to have access to my rooms, she would answer for nothing; — that the ‘shrine’ would be desecrated, etc.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-121" href="#sec-07-fn-121" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-121" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Damodar, a Hindu who had become a Theosophist and was one of Madame Blavatsky’s secretaries, had the keys of the Occult Room and the Shrine.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-122" href="#sec-07-fn-122" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-122" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> Only these three had free access to the penetralia at headquarters. The affairs of the society were left by Colonel Olcott, the President, in the hands of a Committee of seven.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-277"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="277" data-folio="237" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n276_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 237">[p. 237]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>b.</em> On the 29th of February one member of this Committee, Dr. Hartmann by name, arrived at headquarters; and two or three days later a meeting of the Committee was held. In order that they might sit in quiet, Dr. Hartmann proposed that they should meet in Madame Blavatsky's room upstairs; but, to his amazement, the Coulombs refused to give them admittance. The consequence was a bitter quarrel between the Coulombs, on the one side, and the members of the Committee and the other residents at headquarters, on the other. Madame Coulomb said that she had many secrets which she would tell, if they continued to molest her.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-123" href="#sec-07-fn-123" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-123" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> She said there were sliding panels in the walls by which phenomena were created, and secret panels in the Shrine, by mean of which the letters from the Master and other things were introduced from Madame Blavatsky's room behind. She also talked of the money which she had lent Madame Blavatsky in Egypt and which had not been repaid.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-124" href="#sec-07-fn-124" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-124" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Hence Dr. Hartmann and others wrote to Madame Blavatsky and Colonel Olcott, complaining of the Coulombs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">So serious did matters become in the meantime, that the Committee decided to impeach them in an informal manner, and expel them from headquarters. But on March 22nd, while they were drawing up the charges against them, Damodar laid before them a letter,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-125" href="#sec-07-fn-125" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-125" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> which he declared had been brought from Koot Hoomi by a chela in his astral body, advising them not to turn out the Coulombs. Naturally, the Committee were rather upset to find such an authority interfering to save the traitors. Yet, in the face of a message direct from the Master, they dared not turn <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-278" data-page="278" data-folio="238" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n277_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 238">[p. 238]</button> them out of doors. Consequently, as Dr. Hartman says, an armistice was concluded with them.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After the peace was patched up, the Coulombs, Mr. Lane Fox and Damodar went to Ootacamund for a holiday. Meanwhile, the letters despatched early in March, reached the founders in Paris; and they replied, in letters written on the 1st and 2nd of April, to the Coulombs and to others. These letters reached Madras on the 25th of April. On the 26th, the very day when the mail from Europe reached Ootacamund, a letter<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-126" href="#sec-07-fn-126" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-126" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> purporting to come from the Master, and directed to Dr. Hartmann, was forwarded to the latter by Damodar, from Ootacamund. This letter said that the Coulombs were plotting. Therefore, when they returned from Ootacamund, the Committee decided to expel them. On the 17th of May, M. Coulomb gave up the keys he held, and several of the sliding doors and panels which Madame Coulomb had talked about were discovered.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-127" href="#sec-07-fn-127" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-127" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> On the 23rd of May they were finally forced to leave headquarters.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>c.</em> We now turn to Europe for a moment. The Theosophical Society had by this time attained so much notoriety that the London Society for Psychical Research appointed, in May, 1884,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-128" href="#sec-07-fn-128" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-128" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> a Committee for the taking of such evidence as to the alleged phenomena as might be offered by members of that body at the time in England, or as could be collected elsewhere. The journey of the founders to Europe thus came at a very fortunate time, and the Research Society took full advantage of it.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-129" href="#sec-07-fn-129" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-129" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>d.</em> On the 9th of August Madame Coulomb called on the Editor of <em>The Madras Christian College Magazine</em>, and placed in his hands some forty letters, and asked him whether he cared to publish them, as they contained sufficient evidence <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-279" data-page="279" data-folio="239" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n278_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 239">[p. 239]</button> to expose the fraudulent nature of the phenomena which had made so much stir in India.  The Editor asked for a few days to look into the matter.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-130" href="#sec-07-fn-130" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-130" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A few days later the General Council of the Theosophical Society through their Chairman, Dr. Hartmann, sent out a circular letter of inquiry to a number of Theosophists who had visited headquarters, asking them what they knew about the Shrine.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-131" href="#sec-07-fn-131" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-131" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Meantime the Editor of the <em>Christian College Magazine</em> was examining the documents left in his hands.  Most of them were letters from Madame Blavatsky to Madame Coulomb, but there were several other things, a letter from Mrs. Carmichael (the wife of an Indian Civilian) to Madame Blavatsky with a letter to Madame Coulomb written on the back, a receipt for a telegram, etc.  The Editor submitted the documents to the most skilled opinion available in Madras, among others to certain bankers, and they pronounced them genuine.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-132" href="#sec-07-fn-132" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-132" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>  But the letters authenticated themselves.  No one could look through them and believe them to be forgeries.  The question of the handwriting was quite a subordinate one.  The letters contained scores of references to leading Hindus and Government Officials all over India with details of what happened when Madame Blavatsky was in their houses and when she met them casually.  No forger would have dared to invent such details.  If they had been forged, a few personal inquiries would have at once exposed them.  The style was also Madame Blavatsky’s, brilliant, vivacious, full of surprises and sudden changes.  The documents were thus manifestly genuine.  As they contained numerous instructions to Madame Coulomb for the production of phenomena, the Editor decided to publish a number of extracts from them, so as to expose Madame Blavatsky and her frauds.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-280"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="280" data-folio="240" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n279_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 240">[p. 240]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Accordingly, an article appeared in the <em>Christian College Magazine</em>, on September 10th,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-133" href="#sec-07-fn-133" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-133" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> containing extracts from some dozen letters, with sufficient comment to make them comprehensible. The letters were almost all in French. The text and the English translation were given in parallel columns. The Editor quoted only such paragraphs as were necessary to prove the fraud, and omitted numerous passages dealing with the private affairs of individuals, both European and Indian; and most of the letters were not used at all.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The publication of this article caused immense excitement throughout India. Most of the newspapers recognized that it was a genuine exposure, but some doubted whether the Editor had not been hoaxed by forgeries. The leading Theosophists, on the other hand, put the whole matter down as a conspiracy on the part of the missionaries.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>e.</em> Mr. W. Q. Judge, who took part in the foundation of the society in 1875,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-134" href="#sec-07-fn-134" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-134" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> was in Europe in 1884, and was sent by Olcott from Paris to Madras.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-135" href="#sec-07-fn-135" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-135" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> He arrived there sometime in May or June.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>f.</em> Dr. Hartmann now drew up as vigorous a defence of Madame Blavatsky as he could and published it, sometime in September, with the title, <em>Report of Observations made during a nine-months' stay at the Head-quarters of the Theosophical Society at Adyar (Madras), India.</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-136" href="#sec-07-fn-136" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-136" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> A rough and inaccurate plan of the chief rooms at headquarters, probably the work of Judge,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-137" href="#sec-07-fn-137" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-137" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> appeared in it. It is reproduced above, plan <em>A</em>, page 234. Hartmann denied that the letters which had been published were genuine, and charged the missionaries with forming a conspiracy against the Theosophical Society. He confesses the existence at <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-281" data-page="281" data-folio="241" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n280_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 241">[p. 241]</button> headquarters of such sliding panels, trapdoors, holes in the wall, etc., as could be used for the production of occult phenomena ; but he asserts, that M. Coulomb made all these after Madame Blavatsky’s departure, in order to ruin her reputation. The whole conspiracy, however, would be unmasked and the innocence of Madame Blavatsky established in a court of law.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">We have noted Judge’s arrival above because of the following grave incident in which he was concerned. The chief facts are given in a written statement by Dr. Hartmann from which we quote the following :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Of the existence of a movable back to the Shrine and a filledup aperture in the wall, none of us knew anything, and although superficial examinations were made, they divulged nothing ; because to make a thorough examination, it would have been necessary to take the Shrine down, and we were prevented from doing this by the superstitious awe with which Mr. Damodar K. Mavalankar regarded the Shrine, and who looked upon every European who dared to touch or handle the “sacred ” shrine as a desecration.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>At about the time when Major-General Morgan sent his invitation to Mr. Patterson to come to headquarters, that examination was made, and it was found that the back of the Shrine could be removed, and on moistening the wall behind the Shrine with a wet cloth, it was found that an aperture had existed, which had been plastered up. . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>I must confess that it seemed to me that if at that inopportune moment this <em>new discovery</em>, to which I then alluded in the papers (see <em>Madras Mail</em>), would have been made public, it would have had a bad effect on the public mind . . .</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>A gentleman who was present, and who shared my opinions, was of the opinion that the <em>Shrine</em> had been too much desecrated to be of any more use, and he burned the Shrine in my presence.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-138" href="#sec-07-fn-138" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-138" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">What they found was that the back of the Shrine consisted of three movable panels, and that there had been an aperture <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-282" data-page="282" data-folio="242" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n281_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 242">[p. 242]</button> in the thin brick partition behind;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-139" href="#sec-07-fn-139" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-139" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> so that there had actually been direct communication between Madame Blavatsky’s room and the interior of the Shrine, precisely as Madame Coulomb had said.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-140" href="#sec-07-fn-140" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-140" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The aperture had been plastered up when Madame Blavatsky sailed for Europe. Among those who examined the Shrine and made the discovery were Dr. Hartmann, Mr. Judge and Mr. T. Vijayaraghava Charloo (known as Ānanda);<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-141" href="#sec-07-fn-141" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-141" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> and it was Judge who burned the Shrine.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-142" href="#sec-07-fn-142" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-142" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> The date of the discovery was September 20th.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-143" href="#sec-07-fn-143" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-143" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dr. Hartmann and Theosophists generally have always maintained that the sliding panels in the back of the Shrine and the hole in the wall behind it, which made it possible to get access to the Shrine surreptitiously from Madame Blavatsky’s room, were made by M. Coulomb <em>after</em> Madame Blavatsky sailed for Europe in February, 1884. It is passing strange that they destroyed the Shrine, if they were really convinced that M. Coulomb had made these arrangements in order to ruin Madame Blavatsky. Why did they not preserve this most notable piece of evidence of his villainy?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The truth is that it is totally impossible to believe that the sliding panels in the Shrine and the hole in the wall were made by M. Coulomb <em>after</em> Madame Blavatsky’s departure; for while the Coulombs had charge of her rooms, Damodar had <em>the keys of the Occult Room and the Shrine.</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-144" href="#sec-07-fn-144" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-144" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> How then could M. Coulomb insert sliding panels in the back of the shrine, and dig a hole through the wall without the knowledge of Damodar? The burning of the Shrine shows that Judge and Hartmann had had some glimpse of this truth. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-283" data-page="283" data-folio="243" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n282_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 243">[p. 243]</button> Clearly they were conscious that no defence of Madame Blavatsky was possible while the Shrine remained in existence.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>g</em>. It is important that Hartmann’s bold promise of a lawsuit should be kept in mind. In making it Dr. Hartmann did not stand alone. Judge was especially bold in promising a full exposure in court;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-145" href="#sec-07-fn-145" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-145" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and Theosophists in every part of India loudly proclaimed that the missionaries would be prosecuted, and their conspiracy laid bare. So strong was confidence at headquarters that again and again it was prophesied that they would rue the day when first they accepted the lying evidence of two dismissed servants.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-146" href="#sec-07-fn-146" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-146" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The London Lodge published a pamphlet in which it was stated that the matter would go to Court; and Madame Blavatsky also stated in an interview with a representative of <em>The Pall Mall Gazette</em> that she was hurrying to India to commence proceedings against the missionaries.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-146-2" href="#sec-07-fn-146" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-146" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But, while this was what she said in public, she wrote in a very different strain to M. Solovyoff. We quote part of her letter. The date is early in October, 1884:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“First of all, you can say to each and all in Paris that since, in spite of all my efforts, in spite of my having sacrificed to the society life and health and my whole future, I am suspected not only by my enemies but even by my own theosophists. I shall cut off the infected limb from the sound body; that is, I shall cut myself off from the society. They have all clutched at the idea with such delight, Olcott and Madame Gebhard and the rest, that I have not even met with any pity. I leave the moral to you. Of course, I shall not depart into the ‘wilderness’ till Olcott, who starts for India by the first steamer, has arranged matters at Adyar, and exposed and proved the conspiracy —they gave the Coulomb woman 10,000 rupees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-147" href="#sec-07-fn-147" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-147" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> as is now proved, in order to destroy the society; but when all this has settled down, then I shall go off, —where, I do not know yet; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-284" data-page="284" data-folio="244" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n283_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 244">[p. 244]</button> it is all the same, besides, so long as it is somewhere that nobody knows.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-148" href="#sec-07-fn-148" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-148" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>h.</em> In October a second article appeared in <em>The Christian College Magazine,</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-149" href="#sec-07-fn-149" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-149" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> in which the missionaries, in reply to Dr. Hartmann’s pamphlet and to other criticisms which Theosophists had raised against them, published a further instalment of letters, and indicated still more clearly the great strength of their position.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>i.</em> So keen was the interest in the Psychical Society on the question of the Theosophical phenomena and of the genuineness of the letters published in <em>The Christian College Magazine</em> that the Committee appointed by them to consider the phenomena determined to send one of their number to India to make careful scientific investigations on the spot. Mr. Richard Hodgson, B.A., of St. John’s College, Cambridge, was sent out at the expense of Prof. Henry Sidgwick. He arrived in Madras on the 18th of December. On the 20th of December Madame Blavatsky and Colonel Olcott arrived at the headquarters in Madras.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The following is Mr. Hodgson’s own statement of his attitude of mind :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>Before proceeding it may be well for me to state that the general attitude which I have for years maintained with respect to various classes of alleged phenomena which form the subject of investigation by our Society enabled me, as I believe, to approach the task I had before me with complete impartiality ; while the conclusions which I held and still hold concerning the important positive results achieved by our Society in connection with the phenomena of Telepathy, — of which, moreover, I have had instances in my own experience, both spontaneous and experimental, and both as agent and percipient, — formed a further safeguard of my readiness to deal with the evidence set before me without prejudice as to the principles involved. Indeed, whatever prepossessions I may have held were distinctly in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-285" data-page="285" data-folio="245" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n284_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 245">[p. 245]</button> favour of Occultism and Madame Blavatsky—a fact which, I think I may venture to say, is well known to several leading Theosophists.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-150" href="#sec-07-fn-150" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-150" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Hodgson’s actions fully bear out his statement. When he arrived in Madras, the Editor of <em>The Christian College Magazine</em> offered him hospitality, but he declined it; and a day or two later the Editor heard that he had gone to reside at the Theosophic headquarters; and there he resided all the time he was in India (nearly three months), except when he went on short visits to places at a distance from Madras. Madame Blavatsky acknowledges frankly that he was friendly to the Theosophist cause when he arrived in India. She writes to M. Solovyoff:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It was he (<em>i.e.</em> Hartmann) who turned Hodgson, the representative sent by the London Psychical Society to inquire into the phenomena in India, from a friend, as he was at first, into an enemy.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-151" href="#sec-07-fn-151" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-151" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Hodgson acted wisely, I believe, in putting up at headquarters. He thus gave Madame Blavatsky, Colonel Olcott and all their followers the fullest possible opportunity of explaining every suspicious circumstance and giving all the evidence they possessed to prove that the letters which had been published were forgeries; while he himself was able to become acquainted with every corner of the rooms at headquarters, except in so far as the Theosophic leaders had destroyed the evidence.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-152" href="#sec-07-fn-152" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-152" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Editor of <em>The Christian College Magazine</em> handed the incriminating letters to Mr. Hodgson for examination, on condition that they should be returned, as they were the property of Madame Coulomb, and were to be handed <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-286" data-page="286" data-folio="246" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n285_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 246">[p. 246]</button> back to her as soon as all danger of a prosecution should have passed away.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-153" href="#sec-07-fn-153" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-153" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Hodgson interviewed the people who supplied the materials for building and repairs, traced the vases, saucers, flowers, etc., which appeared in the phenomena, to the shops or other places whence they came, and endeavoured to fit these facts into the accounts given by those who witnessed the phenomena. He tested all the details of the incriminating letters, cross-questioned witnesses, examined the places referred to, and compared the documents with <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-287" data-page="287" data-folio="247" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n286_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 247">[p. 247]</button> acknowledged specimens of Madame Blavatsky’s handwriting in matters of spelling, phraseology,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-154" href="#sec-07-fn-154" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-154" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> style, etc. No other person, whether Theosophist or not, had the opportunity of examining all the witnesses personally, of seeing all the rooms and other places involved in the matters at issue, and of handling all the documents. Any one who, from a sincere desire to get at what actually happened in these matters, will work patiently and carefully through the multitude of details supplied in all the sources, will realize with what extreme honesty and with what infinite pains Hodgson collected and sifted the evidence.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As he proceeded with this persistent scientific search for the facts, it became evident that the Theosophic leaders were not trustworthy witnesses, that they contradicted themselves and each other in multitudes of particulars. Each new piece of cross-questioning on Mr. Hodgson’s part produced a new version of some occurrence. Madame Blavatsky,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-155" href="#sec-07-fn-155" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-155" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Colonel Olcott,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-156" href="#sec-07-fn-156" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-156" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> Hartmann<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-157" href="#sec-07-fn-157" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-157" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> and Damodar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-158" href="#sec-07-fn-158" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-158" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> all produced a very bad impression.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Here is what happened when Mr. Hodgson asked his first questions about the shrine in December, 1884 :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Madame Blavatsky professed ignorance on the subject, saying she had been unable to discover what had been done with the Shrine. Mr. Damodar and Mr. Hartmann both denied having any knowledge of it, and it was only after repeated and urgent requests to be told what had happened that I learnt from the halting account given by Mr. Damodar and Dr. Hartmann that the Shrine had been removed from the Occult Room (see Plan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-159" href="#sec-07-fn-159" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-159" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup>) into Mr. Damodar’s room at about mid-day of September 20th, that on the following morning, at 9 o’clock, they found the Shrine had been taken away, and they had not seen it since. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-288" data-page="288" data-folio="248" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n287_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 248">[p. 248]</button> They threw out suggestions implying that the Coulombs or the missionaries might have stolen it.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-160" href="#sec-07-fn-160" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-160" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Hodgson questioned every Theosophist who had sent in written answers to Dr. Hartmann about the Shrine and any other one who could throw any light on its history, and in this way gradually pieced together a certain amount of information about it.
