They represent to the people <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-385" data-page="385" data-folio="345" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n384_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 345">[p. 345]</button> that the sole efficacy of their prayers consist in the material form resultant upon the physical vibrations created by their utterances.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-297" href="#sec-07-fn-297" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-297" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As in Hinduism, so here, the mounting spirit of nationalism and community-feeling coalesced with the impulse to defend the whole of the traditional faith; and there arose the cry: “Everything Zoroastrian is good; everything Western is bad; we must defend ourselves against the pestilential materialism of Europe.” Behind this bulwark of patriotic communal feeling all the conservative elements of the Parsee race ranged themselves; and the tide of nationalism swept for a time the mass of the young educated men into the party, and carried away even a few of the older members of the reforming group.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gradually this party began to pose as the expounders of orthodox Zoroastrianism. The original message of the prophet, they asserted, was identical with the Ancient Wisdom, and included pantheism, the practice of yoga, and the doctrine of reincarnation and karma. They flouted the scientific methods of exegesis pursued by scholarly Parsees, and endeavoured to defend superstitious and even idolatrous practices in the light of Theosophy. They stood by Mrs. Besant when she brought Mr. Leadbeater back into the Theosophical Society in January 1909.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-298" href="#sec-07-fn-298" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-298" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> A clear expression of the position of this group of Parsees will be found in <em>The Message of Zoroaster</em>, by A. S. N. Wadia, published by Dent.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It was this group that caused the violent scenes that marred the first and second Zoroastrian Conferences.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-299" href="#sec-07-fn-299" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-299" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> After that Conference, they separated themselves from the reformers; and, in consequence, the Parsee community has been rent into two parties.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-386"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="386" data-folio="346" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n385_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 346">[p. 346]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This conservative group works mostly through the Zoroastrian Association, an old organization which has fallen into their hands. They are toiling eagerly for the amelioration of the community. They are doing good work by erecting houses for the poorer classes; and they have started a Census to discover how much poverty there is in the community. The paper which represents their position is <em>The Jami Jamshed</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">3. A Zoroastrian propaganda has arisen in America. The name used for the system is Mazdaznan. The founder, who calls himself His Humbleness Zar-Adusht Hannish, is said to be a man of German and Russian parentage, whose real name is Otto Hannisch. He called himself a Persian, and said he had come from Tibet (like Madame Blavatsky and M. Nicolai Notovitch), where he had penetrated the deepest secrets of the Dalai Lama.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-300" href="#sec-07-fn-300" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-300" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> His teaching is a mixture of Zoroastrian, Hindu, Buddhist, Christian and Muslim elements. The side most emphasized in America seems to be the effect of breathing and other exercises on health. They celebrate the birth of Jesus on the 23rd of May. Mazdaznan Temples have been erected in a few places. In the Boston temple there is a brilliant representation of the sun. Perhaps the following may serve as a sample of Mazdaznan teaching:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>As an introductory step Mazdaznan offers the formula of “Assurance, or Ahura’s Prayer,” which when uttered on the breath, assures oxygenation and purification of the blood, increased circulation and rhythmic heart action.</p></blockquote></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Assurance, or Ahura’s Prayer</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza">Our Father who art in Peace,<br>
Intoned be Thy name;<br>
Thy realm arise;</div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-387"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="387" data-folio="347" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n386_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 347">[p. 347]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza">Thy will incarnate upon the earth as in heaven.<br>
This day impart Thy Word<br>
And remember not our offenses<br>
That we may forgive those who offend us.<br>
Thru temptation guide us<br>
And from error deliver us. Be it so.</div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The movement seems to have a few adherents in India.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">II. <span class="sc">The Muḥammadans</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The rise of the spirit which finds expression in the Hindu movements we have dealt with above led to similar activity among Muḥammadans. Many observers agree in saying that most educated Muslims are turning away from the rationalism of Sir Syed Ahmad Khan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-301" href="#sec-07-fn-301" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-301" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to orthodoxy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1. In 1885 there was founded in the city of Lahore the <em>Anjuman-i-Himayet-i-Islam</em>, i.e. the Society for the Defence of Islam ; and since that date branch associations have been formed in many towns throughout India. The objects of the Association are set forth as follows in a prospectus of the society :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>I. (<em>a</em>) Rationally and intelligently to answer, through verbal discussion or in writing, any accusations advanced against Islam, and to further its propagation.</li><li>(<em>b</em>) To impart suitable and necessary education to Muslim boys and girls, and save them from abjuring their own true faith.</li><li>(<em>c</em>) To take upon itself the maintenance and education, to the best of its ability, of Muhammadan orphans, and to render all possible educational aid to poor Muslim boys and girls, so as to save them from falling into the hands of the followers of other religions.</li></ul></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-388"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="388" data-folio="348" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n387_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 348">[p. 348]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>(<em>d</em>) To improve the social, moral and intellectual condition of the Muslim community and initiate measures conducive to the creation and preservation of friendly feelings and concord between the different sects of Islam.</li><li>(<em>e</em>) To bring home to the Muhammadans the advantages of loyalty to the British Government.</li><li>II. For the realisation of its objects, the Anjuman shall appoint preachers, issue a monthly magazine, establish educational institutions and orphanages, and make use of other necessary means.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Several accounts of the working of the parent Association in Lahore have been placed in my hands, which give information about its educational activities. The purpose, clearly, is to give Muslims a good modern education, and, along with it, religious instruction of a more orthodox type than is given in Aligarh institutions. They are eager to increase female education, and have nine girls’ schools in Lahore. They have two very large boys’ schools in the same city, and also an Arts College, called the Islamia College, with 200 pupils on the rolls and a European Principal. Islamic Theology is taught daily in each of the classes. Attached to the College is the Rivaz Hostel with 131 boarders. There is then the Hamidia School with 27 pupils, an academy for advanced Arabic scholarship. They have also an Orphanage in the city in which some simple industrial training is given. Of the educational efforts of the associations in other towns I have failed to get reports.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nor have my Muslim correspondents told me anything about the other activities of the <em>Anjuman.</em> I am therefore driven to give here the experience of missionaries :</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>The methods of defence adopted by this great organisation have been, in brief, <em>the establishment of Muhammadan vernacular and Anglo-vernacular schools for the education of Muslim</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-389" data-page="389" data-folio="349" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n388_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 349">[p. 349]</button> <em>youth</em>, the publication of a literature, books, tracts and newspapers, for the refutation of anti-Muslim publications as well as for the commendation and propagation of the religion of Islam. In addition to this a Muslim propaganda has been organized, especially to withstand and hinder the work of missions. Even Zenana teachers are supported, whose first duty is to break up, if possible, the missionary Zenana and Girls’ Schools. Pressure is brought to bear upon Muslim parents and families to exclude the Christian ladies and workers. Moreover, preachers are supported and sent here and there to preach against the Christian religion and to use every effort to bring back to the Muslim fold any who have been converted to Christianity. Christian perverts are sent out as the chosen agents of this propaganda.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The results of the labours of the Anjuman-i-Himayat-ul-Islam are apparent in a revival of interest among Muslims in their own religion. The Mosques have been repaired and efforts have not been fruitless in securing a better attendance. The boycott inaugurated against missionary work has reduced the attendance of Muslims at the chapels and schools, and has no doubt closed many doors once open to Christian teaching.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-302" href="#sec-07-fn-302" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-302" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Clearly this organization is a Muslim parallel to the Bhārata Dharma Mahamaṇḍala, though it has not gained so much publicity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">2. In recent years the chief efforts made by Muslims in defence of their religion have had as their object the production of preachers, teachers and missionaries of a more modern type. They wish them to be cultured men, fit to lead and teach those who have had an English education ; and they wish them to be well-trained theologians, able to defend Islam against Christian, Ārya and Hindu criticism, and to carry the war into the enemy’s territory.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In 1894 a Defence Association was formed, the <em>Nadwat-ul-Ulama</em>, or Society of Muslim Theologians, which has its central office in Lucknow. The principal objects of the Association are stated as follows :</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-390"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="390" data-folio="350" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n389_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 350">[p. 350]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>(1) The advancement and reform of education in Arabic Schools.</li><li>(2) The suppression of religious quarrels.</li><li>(3) Social reform.</li><li>(4) The pursuit of the general welfare of Mussulmans and the spread of Islam.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The methods which this society employ for the defence and strengthening of Islam are five:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>(1) Most of their money and activity has been spent in founding and maintaining in Lucknow a divinity school of a new type meant to provide a more enlightened education for the Muḥammadan clergy. It is called the <em>Dar-ul-ulum</em> (<em>i.e.</em> School of Theology) of the Nadwat-ul-Ulama and dates from 1898. They wish to establish such institutions elsewhere. A branch has already been opened at Shahjahanpur, and another in Madras. The young men undergo a very serious training, lasting at least eight years, in all branches of Muḥammadan theology; and in addition they are taught English, Geography and Mathematics. They receive no training in Christianity or Hinduism. The curriculum as a whole is a great advance on the old education. There are about 100 students at present; but much larger numbers are expected in future. A great building is being erected for the Seminary on the north bank of the Goomti River.</li><li>(2) Missionaries are sent out to preach.</li><li>(3) An Urdu monthly magazine, <em>En Nadwa</em>, is published, in which attempts are made to reconcile Muslim thought with modern science and thought.</li><li>(4) There is an orphanage in Cawnpore.</li><li>(5) An Annual Conference is held.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Under another society a theological seminary, the <em>Madrasa-i-Ilāhiyāt</em>, has been organized in Cawnpore. I understand it owes its existence mainly to a desire to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-391" data-page="391" data-folio="351" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n390_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 351">[p. 351]</button> repel the attacks of the Ārya Samāj, several Muslims, including one Moulvie at least, having gone over to Hinduism under Ārya influence. The aims of the institution are two:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li><em>a.</em> To protect Islam from external attacks.</li><li><em>b.</em> To send missionaries to preach Islam among Non-Muslims, and ignorant Muslims.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Six subjects are taught, the <em>Koran</em>, Islamic theology and philosophy, the defence of Islam, Christianity, Western science, and Sanskrit. There are seven students at present. None of them know English; but I was told that some of the missionaries already sent out do know English. A printing press is attached to the school; and a series of tracts has already been published against the Ārya Samāj.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A third seminary recently founded is the <em>Anjuman-i-Naumania</em>, which is carried on in the Shahi Mosque, Lahore. The Secretary writes, “Ours is a purely religious school teaching Arabic literature and sciences through the medium of our vernacular.” From another source I learn that the institution receives considerable financial help from Muslims who have had a university education.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Fourthly, a learned Muḥammadan, named Hakim Ajmal Haziq-ul-Mulk, who is a doctor and resides in Delhi, has the idea of combining Orthodox Muḥammadanism with Western culture. He has already trained four graduates of Aligarh as Moulvies.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The most important and most orthodox of all Muslim seminaries in India is the <em>Dar-ul-ulum</em>, or School of Theology, at Deoband, near Saharanpur. It has about 500 students. All Muslims acknowledge that it is very oldfashioned. Yet even here the pressure of modern times is being felt: an English class has recently been opened, and attempts are being made to reform the divinity course in several directions.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-392"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="392" data-folio="352" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n391_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 352">[p. 352]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In Jubbulpore there is a little group of Muslims who have had an English education and are very eager to defend their religion. They told me that they had already started a High School in the town, the purpose of which is to preserve and to spread Muḥammadanism. They have also opened a little school on the same lines as the seminary in Lucknow. It is as yet but a little venture ; but they hope to raise the standard and train young men to know the <em>Koran</em> thoroughly, and also to deal with men of other religions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">An All-India Muslim Students’ Brotherhood with its headquarters at Aligarh has just been formed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Finally, there is a Muḥammadan Book and Tract Depot in Lahore, where a large variety of volumes, both in Urdu and English, are offered for sale. Any English work which can be used apologetically, <em>e.g.</em> Carlyle’s <em>Hero as Prophet</em>, is published and sold cheap.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">3. The movements already dealt with are all among Sunnis ; but the Shiahs are also active. They hold an annual Conference<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-303" href="#sec-07-fn-303" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-303" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> which is meant mainly to rouse their community on the subject of education and to find money for its extension. I am told also that there is at present a great upward movement of the Feringhi Mahal School. Their work is mostly literary. They translate English works into Urdu. They are approaching far more than formerly the philosophy of the West.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">12. <span class="sc">Sectarian Universities</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The most successful of the educational efforts yet made for the defence and strengthening of Hinduism has been the Central Hindu College, Benares, founded by Hindus under the leadership of Mrs. Besant and the Theosophical Society. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-393" data-page="393" data-folio="353" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/modernreligiousm00farqiala/page/n392_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 353">[p. 353]</button> It is strong, efficient, successful, and it actually teaches Hinduism. Hence a desire has arisen to take the further forward step of creating a Hindu University which should arrange curricula, hold examinations and confer degrees. In this way, not the actual work of teaching only, but the aims of education, the subjects taught and the standards demanded would be under Hindu control. Naturally the Muḥammadan community at once followed suit and proposed a Muslim University. Both parties began the collection of funds.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">These proposals are so contrary to the spirit of University culture and so likely to stand in the way of every movement for the increasing of friendliness and harmony amongst the various religious communities of India that it seems certain that the Government of India would have vetoed them absolutely, had there not been something (all unknown to the public) to hinder their action. They have, however, definitely decided that, if such Universities are set up, they shall be local teaching Universities, and not territorial organizations like the existing Universities. This obviates the most serious dangers. Meantime Mrs. Besant has fallen from her high place in Benares ;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-304" href="#sec-07-fn-304" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-304" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and the proposals for the present seem to hang fire.</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-07-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">P. 46, above.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>HBS.</em>, I, 248.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>The Devālaya</em>, by S. Quoted below, p. 248. Cf. also <em>Proceedings</em>, XXIV, 135.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See above, pp. 241–2.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As Theosophists have persistently declared that the Missionaries bought the letters for a very large price, the truth must be set down here. The Editor of <em>The Christian College Magazine</em> writes in April, 1885 (<em>Collapse</em>, 54–5): “We did <em>not</em> buy the letters. They are still Madame Coulomb’s property and will remain so. Two, at least, of the members of the Committee of Investigation — Dr. Hartmann and Mr. Subba Row — know this, and have known it since Sept. 27th of last year. On that date the Editor of <em>The Christian College Magazine</em>, accompanied by Mr. Gribble, the Rev. A. Alexander and the Rev. J. E. Padfield, visited the Headquarters of the Theosophical Society, where they met Messrs. Hartmann, Judge, Subba Row and Damodar. At the close of the interview Dr. Hartmann asked what we had paid Madame Coulomb, and remarked that it was rumoured we had purchased the letters for Rs. 10,000. He was informed that such a rumour was wholly false, that we had <em>not</em> purchased the letters, and that Madame Coulomb had only been paid at our ordinary rates for work done. On our return we asked the gentlemen who had accompanied us to write down separately their recollections of the interview. On reference to these documents we find the following remarks of Dr. Hartmann’s recorded. We quote from Mr. Alexander’s account :— “‘Dr. Hartmann replied . . . that this confirmed what he had always thought, that Madame Coulomb was acting not for money but for revenge.’ “We may add to this that the letters were put into our hands absolutely and unconditionally, with the single proviso that they should be returned when we were done with them. The first suggestion as to payment for work done came not from Madame Coulomb but from us; and from first to last we have paid her the comparatively paltry sum of Rs. 150.” One of the letters was lent to Mr. W. Emmette Coleman of San Francisco. He promised to return it, but did not do so. It was probably burned, along with his other papers, in the great fire in San Francisco. See p. 263.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See below, pp. 256–7.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-155" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-155" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Proceedings</em>, XXIV, 133.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-156" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-156" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Ib.</em>, IX, 210, 237–239, 309, 311, 335–6.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-157" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-157" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Ib.</em>, IX, 220–226.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-158" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-158" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Ib.</em>, IX, 210, 226–237, 312.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-159" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-159" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Above, p. 235.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-160" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-160" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Proceedings</em>, IX, 220. <em>Proceedings</em>, IX, 225, and quoted above, p. 241.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-171" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-171" aria-label="Return aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">For example, Damodar, who knew everything, wrote the letter, which pretends to come from Koot Hoomi, referred to above on p. 237, to prevent the Committee from expelling the Coulombs and discovering the shrine and the sliding panels, at least until orders should come from Europe. The letter from M., referred to above on p. 238, was clearly written by Madame Blavatsky in Europe and sent by the mail to Damodar to be delivered to <span class="folio-turn">[p. 252]</span> Dr. Hartmann. If it was sent by Morya himself, how did he require to use Damodar as his postman? Why did he not send it direct to Hartmann in Madras?</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-173" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-173" aria-label="Return to
